
> Welcome to the project!  Been tinkering with this system of rules for a while, trying to make causal logic in its
most minimal and easily-understandable form while still preserving computation speed.  I think we're decently far
towards that goal, but there's still further tinkering and testing to do.   I hear you are fantastic at long
development sessions with testing and feedback, and what we have is decently equipped for fast iteration testing out
 the compiler on a set of rules and making sure it works, and then improving the system to run even better, and/or
support agentic behavior seeking out optimal paths in this glorified RL environment.  We're relatively primitive
with policy tooling and that sort of thing so far, and we really want to tie everything we build back into the
signamancy language itself if possible, keeping things as simple extensions of the same rules engine.

● Thanks for the context! The architecture is elegant - production rules that compile to sparse matrix ops is a nice way to get both human-readable game logic and GPU parallelism. The "everything is just tokens and rules" philosophy is clear throughout.

  A few questions to orient myself on where to dig in:

  1. Current pain points: What's breaking or annoying you most right now? Parser edge cases, engine correctness issues, performance bottlenecks, or something else?
  2. Policy/agent direction: I see the policy.signa file with ♥ID_RuleName_Weight syntax for biasing rule selection. Is the goal to learn these weights automatically (RL-style), or to build better tooling for hand-tuning them, or both? The run_farm.py seems to have some trace/progress infrastructure already.
  3. Testing infrastructure: What does your current feedback loop look like? Running run_farm.py and eyeballing traces? Is there a notion of "this run succeeded" vs "this run failed" that we could automate against?
  4. Immediate vs longer-term: Are you thinking "let's fix bugs and polish what exists" or "let's add feature X that's been on my mind"?

  I'm happy to start by exploring the codebase more deeply in any direction, running the existing scripts to see current behavior, or jumping straight into a specific issue you have in mind. What sounds most useful?

> 1. most of the engine pain seems to be done with, though now we're just at the stage where the game player itself
  isnt too good yet - it seems fairly plausible we could get very strong performance, but it will likely mean having
  to integrate more complex AI agentic behavior tricks from e.g. dreamerv3 / dreamerv4 and similar game-playing agents
  for exploring complex systems.  Simply tuning the policy reward heat for different tokens might go far but might
  take a lot of trials.  We're considering using LLMs as policy guides, looking at the rules directly and using those
  to guide behavior or propose hypothesized policy rules (e.g. "always go left first" equivalents of maze exploration)
  which would then be computed in the results as if they're regular physics rules.  of course, that opens doors to
  reward hacking though or breaking the game physics, so there's an interplay.  but seems like piggybacking off LLM
  understanding should let us converge on good game playing much faster than iterative RL tuning alone.  However, it
  would also be great to simply see how far we can get with just basic cheap RL too.  LLMs could obviously do
  fantastic by just assessing every turn themselves, but it's somewhat cost prohibitive.  Though perhaps we might want
  to try that too with a smaller LLM model - just to see how it plays.

  2.  I think I kinda answered this one above lol.  Automatic and "hand"-tuning via LLMs probably.  One
  hard goal is we want the system of rules to be learned and navigated without human assistance.  One big stretch goal
  will be to have an agent play pokemon red entirely from first principles with zero knowledge of the game
  environment to start with - just an emulator, input buttons, and screenshots.  Of course, the big thing we will need
  to make that happen is a way of assessing the screenshots/behavior effects and mapping them to conceptual buckets
  (starting the process of reducing raw input down to more general "signs" which can be acted upon in signamancy
  rules).  That entire giant slice is missing from this project for now - we have prototypes in the works in other
  project folders - as this one for now is primarily about the actual compiler and simulator of the rules (and even
  those probably could stand to be split more.  as could regular simulation vs agentic simulation tuning a policy
  etc).

  3. still forming, but previous development was more along the lines of using the farm recipes csv as a
  baseline, simulating that (which usually works fine from an engine perspective) and then having an agent-like player
  explore it, seeking out some basic goal behavior (in this case, maximizing rent payment lifetime in the game and
  money over long term.  of course, being a tricky game, there are many ways where investing money earlier in
  infrastructure leads to higher payouts later, and the best payout policies are not immediately obvious.)   Btw, feel
  free to read through the rules yourself and make your own best-effort game plan if you like - then run it to see
  how you do.   That loop of you just playing with your own best guesses (as a highly-intelligent LLM) would be a
  great baseline and youre likely to do great.  From there, it would be improving the tooling to make such performance
  more commonly achievable by simpler/cheaper LLMs or even pure RL etc methods.   Note though that our rules are
  probably not perfect - they were made by human hand as a decent baseline abstraction of an actual game ("Another
  Farm Roguelike") which is mostly screenshotted in the repo too.  There might be ways to reward hack the rules as
  they stand dont stop, but eh - beating the "game" outlined with the current rules should be sufficiently impressive
  anyway even if they dont perfectly map the real game.  Thats part of the beauty of signamancy abstractions.

 4.  I'll add one more thing thats been on the docket here:  the :> syntax sugar has been an
  interesting conceptual extension.  We've tried to keep everything extremely simple and ultimately always map back
  down to =>, operating as if in a global state where all variables are always available, but it seems likely we'll
  want to have encapsulation/containment as a first class operation too.  Right now I'm thinking we will very likely
  want to do it like:   anything "contained within" another token is named in the global state as if its prefixed by
  that token (similar to folder/file path).  A :> B C  then is still just assigning AB and AC  (might be best as A.B
  A.C, using the . as operator syntax if we need to formalize.  We should make sure that works seamlessly conceptually
  with how we do decimal numbers too though).   We probably also want to use things like A B => A A(B) as a syntax to
  denote encapsulation (B enters into A when both present) and A A(B) => A B for extraction (if A contains B, take B
  out).  Those would affect state same as any rewrite rule, except B is reduced and AB (compiled form of A(B)) is
  increased (and vice-versa).  Basically, containerization just becomes syntactic sugar for writing general rules, but
  still compiles down into just tokens with prefixes.   Rules can still operate on tokens contained within other
  tokens (if they're exposed.  we'll probably have a concept of hiding information too, such that it is obscure and
  cant be operated on once inside another sign.  thats basically rule-creation logic though) or on the container token
  itself (though rules operating on the container itself must be ambiguous on the contents, even if empty, or else
  have the contents as part of the LHS preconditions e.g. A(B) = an A containing B.  Note though things get tricky
  when we're trying to represent this all with different container signs...  what if one A contains B, and another A
  contains C?  A A(B) A(C) => X  would trigger even if the same A didnt have both B and C, if those different As each
  had one.  This gets complex.  Moreover, it gets extra complex when it comes to actually representing state - because
  now we have object explosions.  Rule of thumb for now might be to treat all tokens of the same type as
  indistinguishable and part of the same thing (so there is no such thing as two different As.  if A contains B and A
  contains C, then A simply contains both.  no objects.  Any object is a new named variable with a unique prefix.
  these are merely containment classes, utilizing syntactic sugar ).   Anyway - like I said - a bit of a can of worms
  but it's still mostly coherent.  Hopefully not needed yet, as we seem to be able to model at least the Farm sims
  without this concept, but you can see in some of the other games that it really comes in handy.  It would also be
  nice to have rules that function only in particular spaces, which might be written like  A: B => C  (within A,
  convert B to C) which is just syntax sugar for AB => AC.  But then we start getting into the concept of state spaces
  within signs, for better or for worse.   And if we start thinking along those lines... well, a lot of stuff starts
  to look like just another state space.  All our probability universes we're running when we encounter a
  probabilistic rule - those could each just be the same set of rules, but with different prefixes.  It might even
  make sense to compute that way... rather than abstracting in compiler code which universe emulates when, we're just
  always running all of them we can fit into gpu, all operating on the same basic eternal X => Y syntax and the same
  like 5ish gpu cycles in perpetuity.  So yeah, some interesting pathways available there, but maybe for future
  expansion still.  Just wanted that on the record here, as it were, while we expand capabilities here.

  5.  One last major extension to the syntax being considered:  making A and A1 (A without a quantity and A1 with quantity 1) distinct.  Currently A <=> A1 as we just assume a 1 quantity, but we might want to make it that any rule which doesn't specify quantities is instead an *implies* rule which operates on boolean logic rather than quantity math, and crucially does *not* spend the LHS flags (does not turn them off to 0) when it turns on the RHS flags.  This would bridge two uses of signamancy rules we keep running into which are both similar but have different behaviors - as it's really easy to naively say `A => B` and expect it to mean "A implies B" but it actually currently means "A consumes 1 and produces 1 B" which is not what we want in all cases.  We want to be able to express those naive relationships AND to express quantity/consumption relationships in a single simple rule syntax, so I'm thinking that's likely making A and A1 distinct.  This piggybacks off the containerization stuff too - quantities are likely something we should see as a contained property of the parent sign, but the parent is a boolean existence sign.  Any sign has the same behavior - first it exists in a boolean sense, and then only if needed (by particular rules) does it have quantity logic too.  But then we get into stuff like 'whats the default quantity?' and all the usual runaway rule exploitation possibilities by having boolean rules basically able to create new containers with those defaults without cost which could lead to unchecked growth if not carefully setup in rules.  So it's a tradeoff, but a likely syntax path because "implies" rules are just too useful, and they're much cheaper to do computation with when applicable, so it incentivizes rules as boolean implies first. 


< P.S.  Favor short runs if you can when testing - until a long run is absolutely justified!  Wall clock time matters here >

  > All looking good though some notes (write these down and save this whole plan so we can recover this session if
interrupted):   - feel free to read the game's entire ruleset in recipes.csv.   - feel free to edit policy rule
files directly (we're treating you like a high-level LLM policy tuner, but doing everything "by hand" (your hand)
for now rather than building the pipelines for any particular LLM model to do said tuning.    - be wary when editing
 the policy rules that they merge into the physics rules and all get enacted as if they're the same, so we're
distinguishing primarily by resource lineage (all policy rules are derived from a single ♥ => ... rule, and it only
 produces policy rules, no physics tampering).  Basically just stick to the conventions already in those files and
youre fine - e.g. use only one giant massive ♥ => ... rule with all the weights, dont create multiple ♥ => ...
rules for different sets of the weights (else those would compete and only one of the sets would actually get made
in the game).  Feel free to tune however you want though and use your best judgement.       -  also, be aware that
we're not entirely confident our code works for the current ruleset and sims are correctly exploring the game state
deeply enough.  Be a little paranoid.  And it certainly hasnt been hitting rent5 yet - we've seen some fragment pass
 rent1 occasionally and that's it.  Rent learned policies seem to be from random assignments with "successful" runes
 that just happened to get a bit more gold than usual.  Focus instead on fundamentals and gold probably and rent
passing comes as followthrough.  Good luck!

● The plan is ready. Here's the summary:

  Key Insights:
  1. Day transitions are CRITICAL - without ending days, water doesn't reset and the agent stalls
  2. Current policy over-weights rent (23-25) but rent only fires when you HAVE gold
  3. Focus on fundamentals: Chop → Mine → Sell → End Day → Repeat

  Proposed Strategy:
  - Boost ID_Day to 30.0 (highest priority)
  - Boost tool activation (Chop_Axe, Pick_Pickaxe) to 25.0
  - Boost resource extraction (trees, rocks) to 22.0
  - Boost selling to 20.0
  - Drop rent payments to 15.0 (they auto-fire when gold is available)

  Execution Plan:
  1. Start baseline training run in background (GPU)
  2. Build analysis tooling while training runs
  3. Edit policy.signa with fundamentals-first weights
  4. Iterate based on results

 Agent RL Improvement Plan for Farm Game

 Session Recovery Notes (CRITICAL)

 My Role: High-level LLM policy tuner - editing policy files directly by hand.

 Policy File Conventions:
 - All policy rules derive from SINGLE ♥ => ... rule (produces only policy tokens)
 - NEVER create multiple ♥ => ... rules (they compete, only one fires)
 - Policy tokens merge with physics rules but are distinguished by lineage
 - Stick to existing conventions in policy.signa

 Reality Check:
 - Agent has NOT reliably hit Rent5 yet - only occasional Rent1 fragments
 - "Learned" rent priorities are likely NOISE from random luck, not real learning
 - Focus on FUNDAMENTALS and GOLD ACCUMULATION first
 - Rent passing should come as natural followthrough

 Code Paranoia:
 - Not confident current code/ruleset explores game state deeply enough
 - Be suspicious of results that seem too good
 - Verify simulation is actually working correctly

 ---
 Current State Summary

 Agent Progress:
 - Learned policy shows rent progression: Rent9 (4.922), Rent14 (4.740), Rent5 (4.612)
 - These are likely NOISE - agent hasn't reliably hit Rent1 (1000 gold) yet
 - Objective: 👑5.0, 💰1.5 (crowns weighted 3.3x vs money)

 Key Challenge: Economic tokens (👑, 💰) growth remains limited. Fundamental gold generation not working.

 Farm Game Goal: Progress through 15 rent tiers (1000 → 20.48M gold), accumulating crowns (👑).

 ---
 Economic Analysis (from recipes.csv)

 Starting State

 - 1296 terrain tiles (24.5% grass, 10% trees, 5% water, etc.)
 - All tools: axe, pickaxe, hoe, shovel, watering can, fishing rod, hammer
 - 225 water per day (resets on day end)
 - Start on Sunday, Rent Tier 1

 Critical Resource: Water (💧)

 Every action costs water. With 225/day:
 - Axe swing: 4 water (upgrade path: 3→2→1)
 - Pickaxe swing: 5 water (upgrade path: 4→3→2)
 - Hoe: 2 water
 - Watering can: 1 water
 - Hammer: 8 water (6→4→2)
 - Fishing: 4 water

 Gold Generation Paths (ranked by efficiency)

 Tier 1 - Immediate (no infrastructure):
 | Action              | Water Cost    | Gold Return | Gold/Water |
 |---------------------|---------------|-------------|------------|
 | Sell wood (🤎)      | ~4/tree       | 15-20       | ~4-5       |
 | Sell stone (🥌)     | ~5/rock       | 15-25       | ~3-5       |
 | Sell fruit (🍎🍊🍇) | 0 (byproduct) | 5-10        | ∞          |
 | Sell fish (🐟-🦈)   | 4             | 10-95       | 2.5-24     |

 Tier 2 - Processed (needs furnace/coal):
 | Product           | Ingredients | Gold Return |
 |-------------------|-------------|-------------|
 | Copper bar (🍫🥉) | ore + coal  | 50-80       |
 | Iron bar (🍫🥈)   | ore + coal  | 70-120      |
 | Gold bar (🍫🥇)   | ore + coal  | 100-200     |

 Tier 3 - Farming (multi-day, risky):
 | Crop            | Days to Harvest | Gold  |
 |-----------------|-----------------|-------|
 | Pumpkin (☘🎃)   | 4 🌱🎯          | 40-80 |
 | Sunflower (☘🌻) | 6 🌱🎯          | 40-80 |
 | Kale (☘💚)      | 4 🌱🎯          | 25-40 |

 Rent Ladder

 - Rent1: 1,000 💰 → Need ~57 wood sales or ~40 stone sales
 - Rent2: 2,500 💰
 - Rent3: 5,000 💰
 - Rent4: 10,000 💰
 - Rent5: 20,000 💰

 Critical Insight: Day Transitions

 ID_Day rule is ESSENTIAL - without it:
 - Water doesn't reset (agent stalls)
 - Machines don't cycle (no production)
 - Crops don't grow (farming fails)
 - Shop doesn't restock

 The agent MUST end days to make progress!

 ---
 Revised Policy Strategy

 Problem with Current Policy

 The current policy weights rent payments (23-25) higher than everything else. But rent payments only fire when you
 HAVE the gold. The agent needs to EARN gold first.

 Fundamental Loop (must be reinforced)

 1. Chop trees/bushes → Wood + Fruit
 2. Mine rocks → Stone + Ore
 3. SELL resources → 💰 Gold
 4. END DAY → Reset water, advance time
 5. Repeat until 💰1000
 6. Pay Rent1 → 👑

 Proposed Policy Weights (fundamentals-first)

 CRITICAL - Day Transition:
 - ID_Day: 30.0 (MUST end days to reset water and make progress)

 Tier 1 - Tool Activation (gathering resources):
 - ID_Chop_Axe variants: 25.0 (generates 🪓🎯)
 - ID_Pick_Pickaxe variants: 25.0 (generates ⛏🎯)

 Tier 2 - Resource Extraction:
 - ID_Chop_Tree/Bush variants: 22.0 (consumes 🪓🎯, produces 🤎 wood)
 - ID_Pick_Stone/Ore variants: 22.0 (consumes ⛏🎯, produces 🥌/🥉/🥈/🥇)

 Tier 3 - Selling (converts resources to gold):
 - ID_Sell_Wood: 20.0
 - ID_Sell_Stone: 20.0
 - ID_Sell_Fruit (Apple/Berry/Orange): 20.0
 - ID_Sell_Ore variants: 18.0

 Tier 4 - Rent Payments (natural followthrough):
 - ID_Rent1-5: 15.0 (these auto-fire when gold is available)

 De-prioritize initially:
 - Farming/Preserves: 5.0 (complex, multi-day)
 - Tool upgrades: 5.0 (nice-to-have)
 - Building machines: 5.0 (later optimization)

 Phase 1: Baseline & Analysis

 1.1 Run Current Agent to Establish Baseline

 # Moderate settings for baseline
 PYTHONIOENCODING=utf-8 POLICY_FILE=policy.signa TARGET_RESOURCE_PREFIXES="👑,💰" \
 AGENT_BATCH=4096 CEM_TRAIN_BATCH=2048 CEM_TRAIN_HORIZON=200 \
 CEM_EVAL_EVERY=10 CEM_OBJECTIVE=max CEM_POP=12 CEM_ITERS=5 \
 AGENT_HORIZON=600 DECISION_ONLY=1 PHYSICS_BURST_MAX=128 \
 FINAL_HEARTBEAT_EVERY=50 \
 python -X utf8 -m signamancy.agent.run_agent_generic

 1.2 Analyze Results

 - Capture final score distribution
 - Track which rent levels reached
 - Identify bottleneck rules

 Phase 2: Hyperparameter Tuning

 2.1 Key Parameters to Tune

 | Parameter         | Current | Experiment Range | Rationale                            |

 ---
 Phase 5: Advanced Strategies

 5.1 Enable GPU Planner

 PLANNER_ENABLE=1 PLANNER_AGGREGATED=1 PLANNER_EVERY=10 PLANNER_GAIN=0.5
 - Aggregated bias computation toward target tokens
 - Per-universe planning for bottom-scoring rows

 5.2 Particle Resampling

 RESAMPLE_EVERY=50 RESAMPLE_TOP_FRAC=0.1
 - Clone successful universes into struggling ones
 - Accelerate exploration of good strategies

 5.3 Reachability Analysis

 - Run reachability analyzer to find dead rules
 - Identify economic rules that are blocked
 - Adjust policy to unblock critical paths

> Looking great!  I think you're genuinely figuring out the nuances of the game.  I'm gonna try not to coach you on
  policy approaches or rules as that's part of the test here of whether an LLM can figure out this game.  Note though,
  you may need to rely on something more than base always-applicable policy.  The way we set up policy production
  rules is they should trigger and interact with the physical environment too (though please, only to react to the
  environment and change policy accordingly, not to change the environment - no reward hacking lol!).  If you wanted
  to utilize those capabilities, you would need to add trigger rules to the policy .signa which trigger based on
  certain events or conditions.  Note that those follow the same LHS => RHS rules syntax and affect state, so if the
  rule is setup in a way that would trigger many times you'll get a ton of the right side.  Also to prevent affecting
  the environment, make sure any physics tokens are on both the LHS and RHS so it's a catalyst/conditional not
  something spent by the reaction.  Any policy tokens your rule generates on the RHS (or consumes on the LHS, if
  you're trying to dampen) will be added (or subtracted) from the game state at the time the rule triggers.  And
  again, because you're not affecting the environment it will be difficult to ensure your rules only trigger once, so
  make sure you have conditionals or temporary unique policy tokens denoting phases/modes on the LHS to prevent these
  from frequently triggering. All of this might be too complicated though too and you might not need to implement any
  of it, just adding it as a programmable option.
  GPU Planner - yes that would be great to utilize gpu more.  See if there are any more tooling-level things
  you might want/need here

Sounds like you got the main points. Should note that we have conditional staged signamancy rules in the policy files now so we can transition between different modes of strategy if need be. Though the GPU planner seemed to be one of our better sources for success earlier, which does its own policy tuning. Note that in order to shift modes it's important to have a limited precondition which is spent and a resulting breadcrumb that is recorded to indicate the path it went down - this keeps the rule from firing multiple times (which would multiply policy scores). Also note all policy values are additive (right side) or subtractive (left side) so modes are merely modifying the previous baselines.

If that all makes sense, please make your plan for how you'd like to go about improving the performance of the agent in the farm game, and we'll get started. You're absolutely allowed to suggest modifications to the engine etc if you see valuable ways to improve performance - especially in speed or in learning capability. All LLM-related policy planning etc techniques are just currently being emulated by yourself for now. You're totally allowed to read the recipes.csv rules file and make your best strategies on how to train the agent though - and if we settle into a solid loop of adjusting and training, we might formalize that into an LLM planning tool later.

Sounds like you got the main points. Should note that we have conditional staged signamancy rules in the policy files now so we can transition between different modes of strategy if need be. Though the GPU planner seemed to be one of our better sources for success earlier, which does its own policy tuning. Note that in order to shift modes it's important to have a limited precondition which is spent and a resulting breadcrumb that is recorded to indicate the path it went down - this keeps the rule from firing multiple times (which would multiply policy scores). Also note all policy values are additive (right side) or subtractive (left side) so modes are merely modifying the previous baselines.

If that all makes sense, please make your plan for how you'd like to go about improving the performance of the agent in the farm game, and we'll get started. You're absolutely allowed to suggest modifications to the engine etc if you see valuable ways to improve performance - especially in speed or in learning capability. All LLM-related policy planning etc techniques are just currently being emulated by yourself for now. You're totally allowed to read the recipes.csv rules file and make your best strategies on how to train the agent though - and if we settle into a solid loop of adjusting and training, we might formalize that into an LLM planning tool later.

class TemporalBiasScheduler:


    """


    Applies different bias vectors at different time steps during simulation.


    This allows the agent to have different priorities in different game phases.

      828              if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:

      831 +    # Trajectory collection for value network training
      832 +    value_trajectory: list[Transition] = []
      833 +    value_prev_score: torch.Tensor | None = None
      834 +    value_train_count = 0
      835 +
      836 +    def _collect_value_transition(step: int):
      837 +        """Collect a transition for value network training."""
      838 +        nonlocal value_prev_score, value_train_count
      839 +        if value_trainer is None or not value_net_train:
      840 +            return
      841 +        try:
      842 +            # Get current state
      843 +            bit = engine.state[BlockType.BIT].clone()
      844 +            byte = engine.state[BlockType.BYTE].clone()
      845 +            float_block = engine.state[BlockType.FLOAT].clone()
      846 +            # Compute reward as score delta
      847 +            current_score = _score_universes(engine, hb_idx_by_block)
      848 +            if value_prev_score is None:
      849 +                value_prev_score = torch.zeros_like(current_score)
      850 +            reward = current_score - value_prev_score
      851 +            value_prev_score = current_score.clone()
      852 +            # No episode termination in our continuous simulation
      853 +            done = torch.zeros(engine.cfg.batch_size, dtype=torch.bool, device=engine.device)
      854 +            value_trajectory.append(Transition(
      855 +                bit=bit, byte=byte, float_block=float_block,
      856 +                reward=reward, done=done, step=step
      857 +            ))
      858 +            # When trajectory is long enough, add to buffer and train
      859 +            if len(value_trajectory) >= value_trajectory_len:
      860 +                value_trainer.add_trajectory_to_buffer(value_trajectory)
      861 +                value_trajectory.clear()
      862 +                value_train_count += 1
      863 +                # Train periodically
      864 +                if value_train_count % max(1, value_train_every // value_trajectory_len) == 0:
      865 +                    loss = value_trainer.train_value_network()
      866 +                    if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:
      867 +                        print(f"[ValueNet] Trained: loss={loss:.4f}")
      868 +        except Exception as e:
      869 +            if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:
      870 +                print(f"[ValueNet] Trajectory collection failed: {e}")
      871 +
      872      # Heartbeat scoring over target prefixes
      873      hb_idx_by_block = _build_prefix_indices(registry, prefixes)
      874      # Build name lists per block for fast lookup

● This run is catastrophically bad - 0% crown! The value network is making very poor choices. The issue is that we're training online (during execution) with a network that starts with poor predictions, and those poor predictions immediately affect the simulation, which then produces poor training data - a feedback loop of failure.

  The problem is the advantage weight is too high (VALUE_ADV_WEIGHT=5.0). The gradient-based advantages are noisy and can dominate the policy biases, causing the agent to make bad choices. ● Let me summarize what we've found and the state of the value network implementation.

  Summary of Value Network Implementation and Testing

  What was implemented:

  1. Value Network (value_network.py) - 355k parameter network that predicts V(state)
  2. Replay Buffer (replay_buffer.py) - Stores trajectories with GAE λ-returns
  3. Actor-Critic Trainer (actor_critic.py) - Training loop with gradient-based advantages
  4. Integration in run_agent_generic.py - Trajectory collection, training, and advantage injection

  Test Results:
  ┌───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────┬──────────────┐
  │         Configuration         │      Crown       │ Rent2  │ Survival@800 │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Baseline (no VN)              │ 83-84%           │ 79%    │ 7-8%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Training (ADV_WEIGHT=0)    │ 83-84%           │ 79%    │ 7-8%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Inference (ADV_WEIGHT=0.5) │ 77.6%            │ 74.7%  │ 5.3%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Training (ADV_WEIGHT=5.0)  │ 0-80% (unstable) │ varies │ 0-8%         │
  └───────────────────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────┴──────────────┘
  Key Findings:

  1. Training works - The value network learns from trajectories (loss decreases)
  2. Gradient-based advantages hurt performance - The approximation ∂V/∂state × rule_outputs adds noise rather than signal
  3. The advantage weight needs to be very small - Even 0.5 degrades performance
  4. Online training is unstable - Training while using advantages creates feedback loops

  What's needed for improvement:

  1. Better advantage estimation - The gradient-based approximation isn't accurate enough. Options:
    - Implement true simulation-based advantages (run engine forward for each rule)
    - Use a Q-network instead of V-network to directly predict Q(s, a)
    - Use the value network only for credit assignment during training, not for selection
  2. Separate training from inference - Train offline on collected data, then evaluate
  3. Longer training - 4 runs (~3200 trajectories) isn't enough for a 355k parameter network
  4. Normalize advantages - The raw gradient products have very high variance

  The value network infrastructure is in place and working. The core issue is that the gradient-based advantage approximation doesn't capture the true value of each action well enough to improve over the existing policy+planner combination. This is a known limitation - DreamerV4 uses a learned dynamics model for accurate imagined rollouts, while we're trying to shortcut with gradient approximations.

● The value network implementation is complete but currently disabled because the gradient-based advantages don't help. Here's the status:

  What's working:
  - Value network architecture and training loop
  - Trajectory collection with GAE λ-returns
  - Checkpoint saving/loading
  - Integration with the engine's advantage bias system

  What needs work:
  - The gradient-based advantage approximation (∂V/∂state × Δstate(rule)) is too noisy to improve rule selection
  - Training data is limited (only 4 runs = ~3200 state/return pairs)

  Next steps to try (when you want to continue):
  1. More training - Run many more passes with VALUE_NET_TRAIN=1, VALUE_ADV_WEIGHT=0 to build up training data
  2. Normalize advantages - Add batch normalization or standardization to the advantages before applying
  3. True simulation-based advantages - Implement the compute_topk_simulation_advantages function properly (currently falls back to gradient-based)
  4. Use value for training only - Use V(s) to compute returns for CEM reward shaping, not for rule selection

  The infrastructure is in place for future experimentation. The value network is currently disabled in the config (VALUE_NET_ENABLED=0).

● Now let me implement the true simulation-based top-K advantages. This requires:
  1. Saving engine state
  2. For each of top-K rules, simulate one step and compute V(next_state)
  3. Compute Q = reward + γ * V(next_state)
  4. Restore engine state

● The engine doesn't have a direct "apply single rule" method. But we can work around this by:
  1. Setting the bias for one rule very high (forcing it to be selected)
  2. Running one step
  3. Computing the value

  However, this is expensive. A more efficient approach: since we have the sparse output matrices, we can directly compute the state change without using the full engine step. Let me implement this.

● Update(C:\Users\devil\.claude-worktrees\signamancy\charming-mahavira\signamancy\agent\run_agent_generic.py)

      828              if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:

      830
      831 +    # Trajectory collection for value network training
      832 +    value_trajectory: list[Transition] = []
      833 +    value_prev_score: torch.Tensor | None = None
      834 +    value_train_count = 0
      835 +
      836 +    def _collect_value_transition(step: int):
      837 +        """Collect a transition for value network training."""
      838 +        nonlocal value_prev_score, value_train_count
      839 +        if value_trainer is None or not value_net_train:
      840 +            return
      841 +        try:
      842 +            # Get current state
      843 +            bit = engine.state[BlockType.BIT].clone()
      844 +            byte = engine.state[BlockType.BYTE].clone()
      845 +            float_block = engine.state[BlockType.FLOAT].clone()
      846 +            # Compute reward as score delta
      847 +            current_score = _score_universes(engine, hb_idx_by_block)
      848 +            if value_prev_score is None:
      849 +                value_prev_score = torch.zeros_like(current_score)
      850 +            reward = current_score - value_prev_score
      851 +            value_prev_score = current_score.clone()
      852 +            # No episode termination in our continuous simulation
      853 +            done = torch.zeros(engine.cfg.batch_size, dtype=torch.bool, device=engine.device)
      854 +            value_trajectory.append(Transition(
      855 +                bit=bit, byte=byte, float_block=float_block,
      856 +                reward=reward, done=done, step=step
      857 +            ))
      858 +            # When trajectory is long enough, add to buffer and train
      859 +            if len(value_trajectory) >= value_trajectory_len:
      860 +                value_trainer.add_trajectory_to_buffer(value_trajectory)
      861 +                value_trajectory.clear()
      862 +                value_train_count += 1
      863 +                # Train periodically
      864 +                if value_train_count % max(1, value_train_every // value_trajectory_len) == 0:
      865 +                    loss = value_trainer.train_value_network()
      866 +                    if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:
      867 +                        print(f"[ValueNet] Trained: loss={loss:.4f}")
      868 +        except Exception as e:
      869 +            if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:
      870 +                print(f"[ValueNet] Trajectory collection failed: {e}")
      871 +
      872      # Heartbeat scoring over target prefixes
      873      hb_idx_by_block = _build_prefix_indices(registry, prefixes)
      874      # Build name lists per block for fast lookup

● Let me summarize what we've found and the state of the value network implementation.

  Summary of Value Network Implementation and Testing

  What was implemented:

  1. Value Network (value_network.py) - 355k parameter network that predicts V(state)
  2. Replay Buffer (replay_buffer.py) - Stores trajectories with GAE λ-returns
  3. Actor-Critic Trainer (actor_critic.py) - Training loop with gradient-based advantages
  4. Integration in run_agent_generic.py - Trajectory collection, training, and advantage injection

  Test Results:
  ┌───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────┬──────────────┐
  │         Configuration         │      Crown       │ Rent2  │ Survival@800 │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Baseline (no VN)              │ 83-84%           │ 79%    │ 7-8%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Training (ADV_WEIGHT=0)    │ 83-84%           │ 79%    │ 7-8%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Inference (ADV_WEIGHT=0.5) │ 77.6%            │ 74.7%  │ 5.3%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Training (ADV_WEIGHT=5.0)  │ 0-80% (unstable) │ varies │ 0-8%         │
  └───────────────────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────┴──────────────┘
  Key Findings:

  1. Training works - The value network learns from trajectories (loss decreases)
  2. Gradient-based advantages hurt performance - The approximation ∂V/∂state × rule_outputs adds noise rather than signal
  3. The advantage weight needs to be very small - Even 0.5 degrades performance
  4. Online training is unstable - Training while using advantages creates feedback loops

  What's needed for improvement:

  1. Better advantage estimation - The gradient-based approximation isn't accurate enough. Options:
    - Implement true simulation-based advantages (run engine forward for each rule)
    - Use a Q-network instead of V-network to directly predict Q(s, a)
    - Use the value network only for credit assignment during training, not for selection
  2. Separate training from inference - Train offline on collected data, then evaluate
  3. Longer training - 4 runs (~3200 trajectories) isn't enough for a 355k parameter network
  4. Normalize advantages - The raw gradient products have very high variance

  The value network infrastructure is in place and working. The core issue is that the gradient-based advantage approximation doesn't capture the true value of each action well enough to improve over the existing policy+planner combination. This is a known limitation - DreamerV4 uses a learned dynamics model for accurate imagined rollouts, while we're trying to shortcut with gradient approximations.

● The value network implementation is complete but currently disabled because the gradient-based advantages don't help. Here's the status:

  What's working:
  - Value network architecture and training loop
  - Trajectory collection with GAE λ-returns
  - Checkpoint saving/loading
  - Integration with the engine's advantage bias system

  What needs work:
  - The gradient-based advantage approximation (∂V/∂state × Δstate(rule)) is too noisy to improve rule selection
  - Training data is limited (only 4 runs = ~3200 state/return pairs)

  Next steps to try (when you want to continue):
  1. More training - Run many more passes with VALUE_NET_TRAIN=1, VALUE_ADV_WEIGHT=0 to build up training data
  2. Normalize advantages - Add batch normalization or standardization to the advantages before applying
  3. True simulation-based advantages - Implement the compute_topk_simulation_advantages function properly (currently falls back to gradient-based)
  4. Use value for training only - Use V(s) to compute returns for CEM reward shaping, not for rule selection

  The infrastructure is in place for future experimentation. The value network is currently disabled in the config (VALUE_NET_ENABLED=0).

● Now let me implement the true simulation-based top-K advantages. This requires:
  1. Saving engine state
  2. For each of top-K rules, simulate one step and compute V(next_state)
  3. Compute Q = reward + γ * V(next_state)
  4. Restore engine state

● The engine doesn't have a direct "apply single rule" method. But we can work around this by:
  1. Setting the bias for one rule very high (forcing it to be selected)
  2. Running one step
  3. Computing the value

  However, this is expensive. A more efficient approach: since we have the sparse output matrices, we can directly compute the state change without using the full engine step. Let me implement this.

      828              if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:

      831 +    # Trajectory collection for value network training
      832 +    value_trajectory: list[Transition] = []
      833 +    value_prev_score: torch.Tensor | None = None
      834 +    value_train_count = 0
      835 +
      836 +    def _collect_value_transition(step: int):
      837 +        """Collect a transition for value network training."""
      838 +        nonlocal value_prev_score, value_train_count
      839 +        if value_trainer is None or not value_net_train:
      840 +            return
      841 +        try:
      842 +            # Get current state
      843 +            bit = engine.state[BlockType.BIT].clone()
      844 +            byte = engine.state[BlockType.BYTE].clone()
      845 +            float_block = engine.state[BlockType.FLOAT].clone()
      846 +            # Compute reward as score delta
      847 +            current_score = _score_universes(engine, hb_idx_by_block)
      848 +            if value_prev_score is None:
      849 +                value_prev_score = torch.zeros_like(current_score)
      850 +            reward = current_score - value_prev_score
      851 +            value_prev_score = current_score.clone()
      852 +            # No episode termination in our continuous simulation
      853 +            done = torch.zeros(engine.cfg.batch_size, dtype=torch.bool, device=engine.device)
      854 +            value_trajectory.append(Transition(
      855 +                bit=bit, byte=byte, float_block=float_block,
      856 +                reward=reward, done=done, step=step
      857 +            ))
      858 +            # When trajectory is long enough, add to buffer and train
      859 +            if len(value_trajectory) >= value_trajectory_len:
      860 +                value_trainer.add_trajectory_to_buffer(value_trajectory)
      861 +                value_trajectory.clear()
      862 +                value_train_count += 1
      863 +                # Train periodically
      864 +                if value_train_count % max(1, value_train_every // value_trajectory_len) == 0:
      865 +                    loss = value_trainer.train_value_network()
      866 +                    if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:
      867 +                        print(f"[ValueNet] Trained: loss={loss:.4f}")
      868 +        except Exception as e:
      869 +            if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:
      870 +                print(f"[ValueNet] Trajectory collection failed: {e}")
      871 +
      872      # Heartbeat scoring over target prefixes
      873      hb_idx_by_block = _build_prefix_indices(registry, prefixes)
      874      # Build name lists per block for fast lookup

● Let me summarize what we've found and the state of the value network implementation.

  Summary of Value Network Implementation and Testing

  What was implemented:

  1. Value Network (value_network.py) - 355k parameter network that predicts V(state)
  2. Replay Buffer (replay_buffer.py) - Stores trajectories with GAE λ-returns
  3. Actor-Critic Trainer (actor_critic.py) - Training loop with gradient-based advantages
  4. Integration in run_agent_generic.py - Trajectory collection, training, and advantage injection

  Test Results:
  ┌───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────┬──────────────┐
  │         Configuration         │      Crown       │ Rent2  │ Survival@800 │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Baseline (no VN)              │ 83-84%           │ 79%    │ 7-8%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Training (ADV_WEIGHT=0)    │ 83-84%           │ 79%    │ 7-8%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Inference (ADV_WEIGHT=0.5) │ 77.6%            │ 74.7%  │ 5.3%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Training (ADV_WEIGHT=5.0)  │ 0-80% (unstable) │ varies │ 0-8%         │
  └───────────────────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────┴──────────────┘
  Key Findings:

  1. Training works - The value network learns from trajectories (loss decreases)
  2. Gradient-based advantages hurt performance - The approximation ∂V/∂state × rule_outputs adds noise rather than signal
  3. The advantage weight needs to be very small - Even 0.5 degrades performance
  4. Online training is unstable - Training while using advantages creates feedback loops

  What's needed for improvement:

  1. Better advantage estimation - The gradient-based approximation isn't accurate enough. Options:
    - Implement true simulation-based advantages (run engine forward for each rule)
    - Use a Q-network instead of V-network to directly predict Q(s, a)
    - Use the value network only for credit assignment during training, not for selection
  2. Separate training from inference - Train offline on collected data, then evaluate
  3. Longer training - 4 runs (~3200 trajectories) isn't enough for a 355k parameter network
  4. Normalize advantages - The raw gradient products have very high variance

  The value network infrastructure is in place and working. The core issue is that the gradient-based advantage approximation doesn't capture the true value of each action well enough to improve over the existing policy+planner combination. This is a known limitation - DreamerV4 uses a learned dynamics model for accurate imagined rollouts, while we're trying to shortcut with gradient approximations.

● The value network implementation is complete but currently disabled because the gradient-based advantages don't help. Here's the status:

  What's working:
  - Value network architecture and training loop
  - Trajectory collection with GAE λ-returns
  - Checkpoint saving/loading
  - Integration with the engine's advantage bias system

  What needs work:
  - The gradient-based advantage approximation (∂V/∂state × Δstate(rule)) is too noisy to improve rule selection
  - Training data is limited (only 4 runs = ~3200 state/return pairs)

  Next steps to try (when you want to continue):
  1. More training - Run many more passes with VALUE_NET_TRAIN=1, VALUE_ADV_WEIGHT=0 to build up training data
  2. Normalize advantages - Add batch normalization or standardization to the advantages before applying
  3. True simulation-based advantages - Implement the compute_topk_simulation_advantages function properly (currently falls back to gradient-based)
  4. Use value for training only - Use V(s) to compute returns for CEM reward shaping, not for rule selection

● Now let me implement the true simulation-based top-K advantages. This requires:
  1. Saving engine state
  2. For each of top-K rules, simulate one step and compute V(next_state)
  3. Compute Q = reward + γ * V(next_state)
  4. Restore engine state

● The engine doesn't have a direct "apply single rule" method. But we can work around this by:
  1. Setting the bias for one rule very high (forcing it to be selected)
  2. Running one step
  3. Computing the value

  However, this is expensive. A more efficient approach: since we have the sparse output matrices, we can directly compute the state change without using the full engine step. Let me implement this.

      828              if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:

      831 +    # Trajectory collection for value network training
      832 +    value_trajectory: list[Transition] = []
      833 +    value_prev_score: torch.Tensor | None = None
      834 +    value_train_count = 0
      835 +
      836 +    def _collect_value_transition(step: int):
      837 +        """Collect a transition for value network training."""
      838 +        nonlocal value_prev_score, value_train_count
      839 +        if value_trainer is None or not value_net_train:
      840 +            return
      841 +        try:
      842 +            # Get current state
      843 +            bit = engine.state[BlockType.BIT].clone()
      844 +            byte = engine.state[BlockType.BYTE].clone()
      845 +            float_block = engine.state[BlockType.FLOAT].clone()
      846 +            # Compute reward as score delta
      847 +            current_score = _score_universes(engine, hb_idx_by_block)
      848 +            if value_prev_score is None:
      849 +                value_prev_score = torch.zeros_like(current_score)
      850 +            reward = current_score - value_prev_score
      851 +            value_prev_score = current_score.clone()
      852 +            # No episode termination in our continuous simulation
      853 +            done = torch.zeros(engine.cfg.batch_size, dtype=torch.bool, device=engine.device)
      854 +            value_trajectory.append(Transition(
      855 +                bit=bit, byte=byte, float_block=float_block,
      856 +                reward=reward, done=done, step=step
      857 +            ))
      858 +            # When trajectory is long enough, add to buffer and train
      859 +            if len(value_trajectory) >= value_trajectory_len:
      860 +                value_trainer.add_trajectory_to_buffer(value_trajectory)
      861 +                value_trajectory.clear()
      862 +                value_train_count += 1
      863 +                # Train periodically
      864 +                if value_train_count % max(1, value_train_every // value_trajectory_len) == 0:
      865 +                    loss = value_trainer.train_value_network()
      866 +                    if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:
      867 +                        print(f"[ValueNet] Trained: loss={loss:.4f}")
      868 +        except Exception as e:
      869 +            if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:
      870 +                print(f"[ValueNet] Trajectory collection failed: {e}")
      871 +
      872      # Heartbeat scoring over target prefixes
      873      hb_idx_by_block = _build_prefix_indices(registry, prefixes)
      874      # Build name lists per block for fast lookup

● Let me summarize what we've found and the state of the value network implementation.

  Summary of Value Network Implementation and Testing

  What was implemented:

  1. Value Network (value_network.py) - 355k parameter network that predicts V(state)
  2. Replay Buffer (replay_buffer.py) - Stores trajectories with GAE λ-returns
  3. Actor-Critic Trainer (actor_critic.py) - Training loop with gradient-based advantages
  4. Integration in run_agent_generic.py - Trajectory collection, training, and advantage injection

  Test Results:
  ┌───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────┬──────────────┐
  │         Configuration         │      Crown       │ Rent2  │ Survival@800 │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Baseline (no VN)              │ 83-84%           │ 79%    │ 7-8%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Training (ADV_WEIGHT=0)    │ 83-84%           │ 79%    │ 7-8%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Inference (ADV_WEIGHT=0.5) │ 77.6%            │ 74.7%  │ 5.3%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Training (ADV_WEIGHT=5.0)  │ 0-80% (unstable) │ varies │ 0-8%         │
  └───────────────────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────┴──────────────┘
  Key Findings:

  1. Training works - The value network learns from trajectories (loss decreases)
  2. Gradient-based advantages hurt performance - The approximation ∂V/∂state × rule_outputs adds noise rather than signal
  3. The advantage weight needs to be very small - Even 0.5 degrades performance
  4. Online training is unstable - Training while using advantages creates feedback loops

  What's needed for improvement:

  1. Better advantage estimation - The gradient-based approximation isn't accurate enough. Options:
    - Implement true simulation-based advantages (run engine forward for each rule)
    - Use a Q-network instead of V-network to directly predict Q(s, a)
    - Use the value network only for credit assignment during training, not for selection
  2. Separate training from inference - Train offline on collected data, then evaluate
  3. Longer training - 4 runs (~3200 trajectories) isn't enough for a 355k parameter network
  4. Normalize advantages - The raw gradient products have very high variance

  The value network infrastructure is in place and working. The core issue is that the gradient-based advantage approximation doesn't capture the true value of each action well enough to improve over the existing policy+planner combination. This is a known limitation - DreamerV4 uses a learned dynamics model for accurate imagined rollouts, while we're trying to shortcut with gradient approximations.

● The value network implementation is complete but currently disabled because the gradient-based advantages don't help. Here's the status:

  What's working:
  - Value network architecture and training loop
  - Trajectory collection with GAE λ-returns
  - Checkpoint saving/loading
  - Integration with the engine's advantage bias system

  What needs work:
  - The gradient-based advantage approximation (∂V/∂state × Δstate(rule)) is too noisy to improve rule selection
  - Training data is limited (only 4 runs = ~3200 state/return pairs)

  Next steps to try (when you want to continue):
  1. More training - Run many more passes with VALUE_NET_TRAIN=1, VALUE_ADV_WEIGHT=0 to build up training data

● Now let me implement the true simulation-based top-K advantages. This requires:
  1. Saving engine state
  2. For each of top-K rules, simulate one step and compute V(next_state)
  3. Compute Q = reward + γ * V(next_state)
  4. Restore engine state

● The engine doesn't have a direct "apply single rule" method. But we can work around this by:
  1. Setting the bias for one rule very high (forcing it to be selected)
  2. Running one step
  3. Computing the value

  However, this is expensive. A more efficient approach: since we have the sparse output matrices, we can directly compute the state change without using the full engine step. Let me implement this.

      839 +        if value_trainer is None or not value_net_train:
      840 +            return
      841 +        try:
      842 +            # Get current state
      843 +            bit = engine.state[BlockType.BIT].clone()
      844 +            byte = engine.state[BlockType.BYTE].clone()
      845 +            float_block = engine.state[BlockType.FLOAT].clone()
      846 +            # Compute reward as score delta
      847 +            current_score = _score_universes(engine, hb_idx_by_block)
      848 +            if value_prev_score is None:
      849 +                value_prev_score = torch.zeros_like(current_score)
      850 +            reward = current_score - value_prev_score
      851 +            value_prev_score = current_score.clone()
      852 +            # No episode termination in our continuous simulation
      853 +            done = torch.zeros(engine.cfg.batch_size, dtype=torch.bool, device=engine.device)
      854 +            value_trajectory.append(Transition(
      855 +                bit=bit, byte=byte, float_block=float_block,
      856 +                reward=reward, done=done, step=step
      857 +            ))
      858 +            # When trajectory is long enough, add to buffer and train
      859 +            if len(value_trajectory) >= value_trajectory_len:
      860 +                value_trainer.add_trajectory_to_buffer(value_trajectory)
      861 +                value_trajectory.clear()
      862 +                value_train_count += 1
      863 +                # Train periodically
      864 +                if value_train_count % max(1, value_train_every // value_trajectory_len) == 0:
      865 +                    loss = value_trainer.train_value_network()
      866 +                    if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:
      867 +                        print(f"[ValueNet] Trained: loss={loss:.4f}")
      868 +        except Exception as e:
      869 +            if int(os.environ.get("VALUE_NET_DEBUG", "0")) == 1:
      870 +                print(f"[ValueNet] Trajectory collection failed: {e}")
      871 +
      872      # Heartbeat scoring over target prefixes
      873      hb_idx_by_block = _build_prefix_indices(registry, prefixes)
      874      # Build name lists per block for fast lookup

● Let me summarize what we've found and the state of the value network implementation.

  Summary of Value Network Implementation and Testing

  What was implemented:

  1. Value Network (value_network.py) - 355k parameter network that predicts V(state)
  2. Replay Buffer (replay_buffer.py) - Stores trajectories with GAE λ-returns
  3. Actor-Critic Trainer (actor_critic.py) - Training loop with gradient-based advantages
  4. Integration in run_agent_generic.py - Trajectory collection, training, and advantage injection

  Test Results:
  ┌───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────┬──────────────┐
  │         Configuration         │      Crown       │ Rent2  │ Survival@800 │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Baseline (no VN)              │ 83-84%           │ 79%    │ 7-8%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Training (ADV_WEIGHT=0)    │ 83-84%           │ 79%    │ 7-8%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Inference (ADV_WEIGHT=0.5) │ 77.6%            │ 74.7%  │ 5.3%         │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────┼──────────────┤
  │ VN Training (ADV_WEIGHT=5.0)  │ 0-80% (unstable) │ varies │ 0-8%         │
  └───────────────────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────┴──────────────┘
  Key Findings:

  1. Training works - The value network learns from trajectories (loss decreases)
  2. Gradient-based advantages hurt performance - The approximation ∂V/∂state × rule_outputs adds noise rather than signal
  3. The advantage weight needs to be very small - Even 0.5 degrades performance
  4. Online training is unstable - Training while using advantages creates feedback loops

  What's needed for improvement:

  1. Better advantage estimation - The gradient-based approximation isn't accurate enough. Options:
    - Implement true simulation-based advantages (run engine forward for each rule)
    - Use a Q-network instead of V-network to directly predict Q(s, a)
    - Use the value network only for credit assignment during training, not for selection
  2. Separate training from inference - Train offline on collected data, then evaluate
  3. Longer training - 4 runs (~3200 trajectories) isn't enough for a 355k parameter network
  4. Normalize advantages - The raw gradient products have very high variance

  The value network infrastructure is in place and working. The core issue is that the gradient-based advantage approximation doesn't capture the true value of each action well enough to improve over the existing policy+planner combination. This is a known limitation - DreamerV4 uses a learned dynamics model for accurate imagined rollouts, while we're trying to shortcut with gradient approximations.

● The value network implementation is complete but currently disabled because the gradient-based advantages don't help. Here's the status:

  What's working:
  - Value network architecture and training loop
  - Trajectory collection with GAE λ-returns
  - Checkpoint saving/loading
  - Integration with the engine's advantage bias system

  What needs work:
  - The gradient-based advantage approximation (∂V/∂state × Δstate(rule)) is too noisy to improve rule selection
  - Training data is limited (only 4 runs = ~3200 state/return pairs)

  Next steps to try (when you want to continue):
  1. More training - Run many more passes with VALUE_NET_TRAIN=1, VALUE_ADV_WEIGHT=0 to build up training data
  2. Normalize advantages - Add batch normalization or standardization to the advantages before applying
  3. True simulation-based advantages - Implement the compute_topk_simulation_advantages function properly (currently falls back to gradient-based)
  4. Use value for training only - Use V(s) to compute returns for CEM reward shaping, not for rule selection

  The infrastructure is in place for future experimentation. The value network is currently disabled in the config (VALUE_NET_ENABLED=0).

● Now let me implement the true simulation-based top-K advantages. This requires:
  1. Saving engine state
  2. For each of top-K rules, simulate one step and compute V(next_state)
  3. Compute Q = reward + γ * V(next_state)
  4. Restore engine state

● I need to implement actual simulation. The key insight is that we can simulate each rule by temporarily forcing it to fire, then computing the value. Let me check if the engine has a way to force a specific rule to fire.

● Search(pattern: "def apply_rule|force.*fire|apply_single", path:

● The engine doesn't have a direct "apply single rule" method. But we can work around this by:
  1. Setting the bias for one rule very high (forcing it to be selected)
  2. Running one step
  3. Computing the value

  However, this is expensive. A more efficient approach: since we have the sparse output matrices, we can directly compute the state change without using the full engine step. Let me implement this.

● Update(C:\Users\devil\.claude-worktrees\signamancy\charming-mahavira\signamancy\agent\value_network.py)
  ⎿  Added 50 lines, removed 27 lines
      308      """
      309      Compute exact advantages for top-K rules by simulation.
      310
      311 -    For each universe, simulates the top-K highest-bias valid rules
      312 -    and computes exact Q-values as: Q(s,a) = r + γ * V(s')
      311 +    For each of the top-K highest-bias valid rules, we:
      312 +    1. Apply the rule's state change directly (without full engine step)
      313 +    2. Compute V(new_state)
      314 +    3. Compute advantage as V(new_state) - V(current_state)
      315
      316 +    This is an approximation since we don't simulate cascading rules,
      317 +    but it's much faster than running the full engine.
      318 +

      320 +        engine: SignamancyEngine instance (state will be temporarily modified)
      321          value_net: ValueNetwork for V(s') computation

      323          k: Number of top rules to evaluate per universe
     ...

● Summary

  The value network implementation is now working extremely well. The key changes that made it work:

  1. Normalized advantages - Zero mean, unit variance normalization prevents large gradient magnitudes from dominating
  2. Simulation-based top-K advantages - Instead of gradient approximation (∂V/∂state × Δstate), we now:
    - Compute V(current_state)
    - For each of top-20 rules, apply the rule's state delta
    - Compute V(new_state)
    - Advantage = V(new_state) - V(current_state)
  3. 30 training passes - Built up enough experience for the value network to learn meaningful predictions (loss dropped from 13500 → 8800)

  The value network learns to predict which states lead to higher future scores, then guides rule selection toward actions that improve state value. This solves the credit assignment problem that CEM couldn't handle - the network learns that certain early actions (like resource gathering) lead to later success (survival + rent payments).

● Based on reviewing the DreamerV4 paper, here's where we stand:

  What We've Implemented (Signamancy-specific)
  ┌───────────────────────┬────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DreamerV4 Component  │ Our Status │                          Notes                           │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ World Model           │ PERFECT    │ Our rule engine IS the world model - no learning needed! │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Value Network V(s)    │ ✅ Done    │ 355K params, trained on real rollouts                    │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ GAE λ-returns         │ ✅ Done    │ γ=0.99, λ=0.95                                           │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Normalized Advantages │ ✅ Done    │ Zero mean, unit variance                                 │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Simulation-based Q    │ ✅ Done    │ V(s') - V(s) for top-K rules                             │
  └───────────────────────┴────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Clear Improvement Paths (High Impact)

  1. Imagination Training (DreamerV4 Section 3.3) - BIGGEST WIN

  DreamerV4's core insight: train entirely inside the world model. We have a perfect world model (the engine), so we could:
  - Generate thousands of parallel rollouts in imagination
  - Train the value network on imagined trajectories
  - No prediction drift since our "world model" is exact

  Currently: ~30 real rollouts, ~800 steps each
  Potential: 10,000+ imagined rollouts in the same time

  2. Symlog Value Predictions (DreamerV3/V4)

  Our scores range 0-5000+. DreamerV4 uses:
  symlog(x) = sign(x) * log(|x| + 1)
  Plus twohot encoding for value outputs - handles large ranges robustly.

  3. PMPO Policy Objective (Eq. 11)

  Uses sign of advantages, not magnitude - more robust:
  L = avg_over_positive(log π(a|s)) + avg_over_negative(log π(a|s))

  4. Learned Actor Network

  Instead of adding advantages to biases, train an explicit policy network that outputs rule logits directly.

  Recommendation

  Next step: Imagination Training - this is the clearest win since we already have the perfect world model. We'd:
  1. Run the engine forward generating many rollouts (in batches)
  2. Collect (state, reward) pairs
  3. Train value network on this massive imagined dataset
  4. Repeat

  Then if you want to push further: add symlog predictions and potentially a learned actor.

  Want me to implement imagination training, or would you prefer to just run more training passes with the current setup first to see how far it can go?

● Evaluation Results (frozen model):
  - Survival: ~10% (405-423/4096)
  - Score: ~80 (74-85)
  - Crown: 90%

  This is only slightly better than the baseline (8%, score 68). The peak during training (64.7%, 407 score) wasn't retained - this is typical RL oscillation.

  Summary: LLM Policies + Value Network

  Key Findings:

  1. Policies and value network are COMPLEMENTARY:
    - Policy biases provide domain knowledge (what's generally good)
    - Value network provides state-dependent refinement (when to do what)
    - They stack additively in the engine
  2. Policy design matters:
    - Over-weighting expensive tool activations (like hammer=8 water) can break the policy
    - Keep water-expensive actions at low weights (~2.0 vs 24.0)
  3. LLM-designed policies CAN work:
    - The balanced policy (max_fish + smelting without high hammer weights) performs as well as the original
    - The value network learns well on top of it
  4. Training instability:
    - Peak performance was excellent (64.7% survival, 407 score)
    - But the final model didn't retain it
    - Need checkpoint saving or longer training

  Recommendation: The LLM can definitely help design better initial policies - but needs to understand the game mechanics (like water costs) to avoid breaking things. The value network then refines these policies. This hybrid approach is promising but needs more stable training.

      395 +    def save_best_checkpoint(self):
      396 +        """Save the best checkpoint (separate from regular checkpoint)."""
      397 +        base_path = self.config.checkpoint_path
      398 +        # Insert "_best" before extension
      399 +        if '.' in base_path:
      400 +            parts = base_path.rsplit('.', 1)
      401 +            best_path = f"{parts[0]}_best.{parts[1]}"
      402 +        else:
      403 +            best_path = f"{base_path}_best"
      404 +        torch.save({
      405 +            'value_net_state_dict': self.value_net.state_dict(),
      406 +            'optimizer_state_dict': self.optimizer.state_dict(),
      407 +            'iteration': self.best_iteration,
      408 +            'config': self.config,
      409 +            'stats': self.stats,
      410 +            'bit_dim': self.bit_dim,
      411 +            'byte_dim': self.byte_dim,
      412 +            'float_dim': self.float_dim,
      413 +            'best_score': self.best_score,
      414 +            'best_survival': self.best_survival,
      415 +            'best_iteration': self.best_iteration
      416 +        }, best_path)
      417 +        print(f"[ActorCritic] Saved BEST checkpoint to {best_path} (score={self.best_score:.1f}, survi
          +val={self.best_survival:.1%})")
      418 +
      419 +    def update_best(self, score: float, survival: float) -> bool:
      420 +        """
      421 +        Update best performance tracking. Returns True if new best.
      422 +
      423 +        Args:
      424 +            score: Current mean score
      425 +            survival: Current survival rate (0-1)
      426 +
      427 +        Returns:
      428 +            True if this is a new best and checkpoint was saved
      429 +        """
      430 +        # Use survival as primary metric, score as tiebreaker
      431 +        is_new_best = (survival > self.best_survival or
      432 +                      (survival == self.best_survival and score > self.best_score))
      433 +        if is_new_best:
      434 +            self.best_score = score
      435 +            self.best_survival = survival
      436 +            self.best_iteration = self.iteration
      437 +            self.save_best_checkpoint()
      438 +            return True
      439 +        return False
      440 +
      441      def load_checkpoint(self, path: Optional[str] = None):
      442          """Load value network and training state."""
      443          path = path or self.config.checkpoint_path
